In February and March 2014, [Russia](25391.md) invaded the [Crimean Peninsula](163045.md) of [Ukraine](31750.md), [occupied](71944314.md) it, and then [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexed) it after an illegitimate [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum). This took place amid the uncertainty that immediately followed Ukraine's [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity).[^cite_note-36] The invasion marked the beginning of the [Russo-Ukrainian war](42085878.md). Crimea's annexation has been described as an instance of [modern Russian imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_neo-imperialism).[^cite_note-37] It was the first time since [World War II](32927.md) that a European country had annexed part of another,[^cite_note-38] and some analysts see the annexation as the beginning of a [Second Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cold_War) between Russia and the West.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-D'Anieri-2023-40]

The [events in Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) that [ousted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity#Removal_of_Yanukovych) Ukrainian president [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) on 22 February 2014 sparked both pro- and anti-separatist[^cite_note-41] [demonstrations in Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2014_Crimean_crisis#February_23). At the same time, Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) told his security chiefs to begin work on "returning Crimea to Russia". On 27 February, [Russian special forces without insignia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_War\))[^cite_note-42] began seizing strategic sites across Crimea.[^cite_note-time1-43][^cite_note-reuters1-44] Russia denied involvement at the time,[^cite_note-auto1-45] but Putin later admitted that they were Russian troops.[^cite_note-revea-46] As the armed men [occupied Crimea's parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_the_Crimean_Parliament), it dismissed the [Crimean government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_of_Crimea), installed the pro-Russian [Aksyonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Aksyonov) government, and announced a [referendum on Crimea's status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum). The referendum was held under Russian occupation and did not have an option to keep the *status quo*. According to the Russian-installed authorities, the result was overwhelmingly in favor of joining Russia. The next day, 17 March 2014, Crimea's authorities [declared independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_the_Republic_of_Crimea) and requested to join Russia.[^cite_note-Washington_Post-47][^cite_note-zakon-48] Russia incorporated Crimea on 18 March 2014 as the [Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(Russia\)) and [federal city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_cities_of_Russia) of [Sevastopol](51587.md).[^cite_note-GovNo-49][^cite_note-revea-46] Russia also claimed an [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) in the [Black Sea](3386.md) around Crimea. This zone is three times bigger than the peninsula and holds vast [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_in_Ukraine) and [oil reserves](77988708.md).[^cite_note-Broad_NYT-50] Russia militarized the peninsula and warned against any intervention.[^cite_note-51]

Ukraine and [many other countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation) condemned the annexation as breaking [international law](8195726.md) and Russian agreements to respect the [territorial integrity](31335.md) of Ukraine. The [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) rejected the referendum and annexation, adopting [a resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_68/262) affirming the "territorial integrity of Ukraine within its internationally recognised borders",[^cite_note-CCTV_UNR_C=U-52][^cite_note-UN_A_RES_68_262-53] and deeming Crimea to be under Russian occupation.[^cite_note-54] The other members of the [G8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_of_Eight) suspended Russia from the group[^cite_note-55] and [imposed sanctions](43449324.md) on Russia. [NATO](21133.md) also suspended [co-operation with Russia](19066574.md).[^cite_note-end_coop-56]

The Russian government opposes the "annexation" label, with Putin defending the referendum as complying with the principle of the [self-determination](29269.md).[^cite_note-\:0-57][^cite_note-58] In his [speech marking the annexation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_speech_of_Vladimir_Putin), Putin said Crimea had always been Russian and should never have become part of an independent Ukraine.[^cite_note-59]

## Other names

### In Ukraine

The names of the Crimean annexation vary. In Ukraine, the annexation is known as the **temporary occupation of the Autonomous Republic of Crimea and Sevastopol by Russia** ([Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language): тимчасова окупація Автономної Республіки Крим і Севастополя Росією, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Ukrainian): *tymchasova okupatsiia Avtonomnoi Respubliky Krym i Sevastopolia Rosiieiu*), the **illegal occupation of the Autonomous Republic of Crimea**, the **fall of Crimea**, and the **invasion of Crimea**.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63]

### In Russia

In the Russian Federation, it is also known as the **accession of Crimea to the Russian Federation** ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): присоединение Крыма к Российской Федерации, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Russian): *prisoyedineniye Kryma k Rossiyskoy Federatsii*), the **return of Crimea** ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): возвращение Крыма, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Russian): *vozvrashcheniye Kryma*), and the **reunification of Crimea**.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65]

Main article: [2014 pro-Russian unrest in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_pro-Russian_unrest_in_Ukraine)

See also: [1783 Russian annexation of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1783_Russian_annexation_of_Crimea), [Crimea in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimea_in_the_Soviet_Union), and [1954 transfer of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_transfer_of_Crimea)

Crimea was part of the [Crimean Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate) from 1441 until it was [annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1783_Russian_annexation_of_Crimea) by the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) in 1783 by a decree of [Catherine the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_II).[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68]

After the downfall of [Russian empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_empire) in [1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) during the first stages of the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War) there were a series of short-lived independent governments ([Crimean People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_People's_Republic), [Crimean Regional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Regional_Government), [Crimean SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Socialist_Soviet_Republic)). They were followed by [White Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_movement) governments ([General Command of the Armed Forces of South Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Command_of_the_Armed_Forces_of_South_Russia) and later [South Russian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Russian_Government)).

In October 1921, the Bolshevik [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) gained control of the peninsula and instituted the [Crimean Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) as a member of Russian Federation. In the following year Crimea joined the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) as a part of Russia (the [RSFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSFSR)).

After the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War) and the 1944 [deportation of all of the indigenous Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_the_Crimean_Tatars) by the Soviet government, the [Crimean ASSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_ASSR) was stripped of its autonomy in 1946 and downgraded to the [status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Oblast) of an [oblast](646268.md) of the Russian SFSR. In 1954, the Crimean Oblast was [transferred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_transfer_of_Crimea) from the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR) to the [Ukrainian SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_SSR) by decree of the [Presidium of the Supreme Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidium_of_the_Supreme_Soviet) of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) to commemorate the 300th anniversary of [Ukraine's union with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Pereyaslav).[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] In 1989, under [Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev)'s [perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika),[^cite_note-71] the Supreme Soviet declared that the deportation of the [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) under [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) had been illegal[^cite_note-72] and the mostly [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) ethnic group was allowed to return to Crimea.[^cite_note-73]

In 1990, the [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_\(council\)) of the Crimean Oblast proposed the restoration of the Crimean ASSR.[^cite_note-74] The oblast conducted a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Crimean_sovereignty_referendum) in 1991, which asked whether Crimea should be elevated into a signatory of the [New Union Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Union_Treaty) (that is, become a [union republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union) on its own). By that time, though, the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) was well underway. The Crimean ASSR was restored for less than a year as part of Ukrainian SSR before the restoration of [Ukrainian independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Ukrainian_independence_referendum).[^cite_note-75] Newly independent Ukraine maintained Crimea's [autonomous status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Republic_of_Crimea),[^cite_note-76] while the [Supreme Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) affirmed the peninsula's "sovereignty" as a part of Ukraine.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]

The confrontation between the government of Ukraine and Crimea deteriorated between 1992 and 1995. In May 1992 the regional parliament declared an independent "Crimean republic."[^cite_note-sasse-79] In June 1992, the parties reached a compromise, that Crimea would have considerable autonomy but remain part of Ukraine.[^cite_note-80] [Yuri Meshkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Meshkov), a leader of the Russian movement was elected President of Crimea in 1994 and his party won a majority in the regional parliamentary elections in the same year. The pro-Russian movement was then weakened by internal disagreements and in March 1995 the Ukrainian government gained the upper hand. The office of the elected President of Crimea was abolished and a loyal head of region was installed instead of Meshkov; the 1992 constitution and a number of local laws were repealed.[^cite_note-sasse-79] According to [Gwendolyn Sasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwendolyn_Sasse) the conflict was defused due to Crimea's multi-ethnic population, fractures within the pro-Russian movement, Kyiv's policy of avoiding escalation and the lack of active support from Russia.[^cite_note-sasse-79]

During the 1990s, the serious dispute over control of the [Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet) and Crimean naval facilities, as well as the status of Crimea in whole were [source of tensions](17446303.md) between Russia and Ukraine.[^cite_note-81] In 1992, [Vladimir Lukin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lukin), then chairman of the [Russian Duma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Duma)'s Committee on Foreign Affairs, suggested that to pressure Ukraine to give up its claim to the Black Sea Fleet, Russia should question Ukrainian control over Crimea.[^cite_note-82] In 1998 the [Partition Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_Treaty_on_the_Status_and_Conditions_of_the_Black_Sea_Fleet) divided the fleet and gave Russia a naval base in Sevastopol, and the [Treaty of Friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian%E2%80%93Ukrainian_Friendship_Treaty) recognized the inviolability of existing borders. However, in [2003 Tuzla Island conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Tuzla_Island_conflict) issues over maritime border resurfaced.

In September 2008, the Ukrainian Foreign Minister [Volodymyr Ohryzko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volodymyr_Ohryzko) accused Russia of giving [Russian passports](8887086.md) to the population in Crimea, and described it as a "real problem", given Russia's declared policy of military intervention abroad to protect Russian citizens.[^cite_note-83]

On 24 August 2009, [anti-Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Ukrainian_sentiment) demonstrations were held in Crimea by ethnic Russian residents. [Sergei Tsekov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Tsekov) (of the [Russian Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Bloc_\(party\))[^cite_note-84] and then deputy speaker of the [Crimean parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_parliament)[^cite_note-85]) said then that he hoped that Russia would treat Crimea the same way as it had treated [South Ossetia](318381.md) and [Abkhazia](18933375.md).[^cite_note-86] Crimea is populated by an [ethnic Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russians_in_Ukraine) majority and a minority of both ethnic [Ukrainians](145144.md) and Crimean Tatars, and thus [demographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Ukraine) possessed one of Ukraine's largest ethnically Russian populations.[^cite_note-87]

As early as in 2010, some analysts already speculated that the Russian government had [irredentist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irredentist) plans. [Taras Kuzio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taras_Kuzio) said that "Russia has an even more impossible time recognizing Ukraine's sovereignty over the Crimea and the port of [Sevastopol](51587.md) – as seen by public opinion in Russia, statements by politicians, including members of the ruling United Russia party, experts and journalists".[^cite_note-88] In 2011, William Varettoni wrote that "Russia wants to annex Crimea and is merely waiting for the right opportunity, most likely under the pretense of defending Russian brethren abroad".[^cite_note-89]

### Euromaidan and the Revolution of Dignity

Main articles: [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) and [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity)



[Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) in Kyiv, 29 December 2013

The [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) protest movement began in [Kyiv](585629.md) in late November 2013 after President [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych), of the [Party of Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Regions), failed to sign the [Ukraine–European Union Association Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93European_Union_Association_Agreement).[^cite_note-90] Yanukovych won[^cite_note-yanukovych-91] the [2010 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Ukrainian_presidential_election) with strong support from voters in the [Autonomous Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Republic_of_Crimea) and [southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Ukraine) and [eastern Ukraine](13065940.md). The Crimean autonomous government strongly supported Yanukovych and condemned the protests, saying they were "threatening political stability in the country". The [Crimean autonomous parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) said that it supported the [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Azarov_government)'s decision to suspend negotiations on the pending association agreement and urged Crimeans to "strengthen friendly ties with Russian regions".[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94]

On 4 February 2014, the Presidium of the Supreme Council "promised" to consider holding a referendum on the peninsula's status. Speaker [Vladimir Klychnikov](https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%9A%D0%BB%D1%8B%D1%87%D0%BD%D0%B8%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B2,%20%D0%92%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80%20%D0%9D%D0%B8%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B5%D0%B2%D0%B8%D1%87) asked to appeal to the Russian government to "guarantee the preservation of Crimean autonomy".[^cite_note-Chronicles_of_Alienation-95][^cite_note-96] The [Security Service of Ukraine](709222.md) (SBU) responded by opening a [criminal case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_case) to investigate the possible "subversion" of Ukraine's territorial integrity.[^cite_note-97] On 20 February 2014, during a visit to Moscow, [Chairman of the Supreme Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) [Vladimir Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Konstantinov_\(politician\)) stated that the [1954 transfer of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_transfer_of_Crimea) from the [Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) to the [Ukrainian Soviet Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) had been a mistake.[^cite_note-Chronicles_of_Alienation-95]

The Euromaidan protests came to a head in late February 2014, and Yanukovych and many of his ministers fled the capital on 22 February.[^cite_note-Ukraine_crisis_timeline_BBC-98] After his flight, opposition parties and defectors from the Party of Regions put together a parliamentary quorum in the [Verkhovna Rada](844993.md) (the Ukrainian parliament), and voted on 22 February to remove Yanukovych from his post on the grounds that he was unable to fulfill his duties.[^cite_note-EnUkrRev-99][^cite_note-interfax1-100][^cite_note-RadioFreeEurope-101] [Arseniy Yatsenyuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arseniy_Yatsenyuk) was appointed by the Rada to serve as the head of a [caretaker government](240683.md) until new presidential and parliament elections could be held. This [new government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Yatsenyuk_Government) was recognised internationally. Russian government and [propaganda](45060755.md) have described these events as a "coup d'état" and have said that the caretaker government was illegitimate,[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105] while researchers consider the subsequent annexation of Crimea to be a true [military coup](25914154.md), because the Russian military seized Crimea's parliament and government buildings and instigated the replacement of its government with Russian proxies.[^cite_note-26436575Putin2-106][^cite_note-idUSBREA4M05420140524-107][^cite_note-PopovaEntangled-108][^cite_note-WilsonCrisis-109][^cite_note-MaidanWar-110]

## Annexation

See also: [Anti-Maidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Maidan) and [Russo-Ukrainian War](42085878.md)

Further information: [Timeline of the annexation of Crimea by the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation)



Crimea (dark green), Rest of Ukraine (light green) and Russia (light red) in Europe

### Russian planning



Russian President Vladimir Putin and his Defense Minister Sergei Shoigu in March 2014

At the end of January 2014, Russian oligarch [Konstantin Malofeev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Malofeev) travelled to Crimea with his head of security, former [Federal Security Service](385990.md) (FSB) officer [Igor Girkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Girkin) (alias 'Strelkov'). Malofeev met with [Vladimir Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Konstantinov_\(politician\)), Chairman of the [Supreme Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea), asking him whether he would be willing to take "more drastic measures" to enforce Crimea's autonomy should there be a revolution. Konstantinov had already met with [Nikolai Patrushev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Patrushev), head of the [Security Council of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_Council_of_Russia), in Moscow in December 2013 where he told Patrushev that if Yanukovych was overthrown, Crimea would be ready to break off and become part of Russia. At the end of January 2014, Konstantinov met with [Vladislav Surkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Surkov), Putin's adviser on Ukraine. On 4 February, Konstantinov appealed to Putin to guard Crimean autonomy and began preparing a "survey" of Crimeans on what its status should be. Surkov visited Crimea on 14 February, where he might have tried to recruit Crimean prime minister, [Anatolii Mohyliov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolii_Mohyliov), for the Russian cause.[^cite_note-Kim-111]

In early February 2014, before the Ukrainian revolution, the Kremlin received a strategy paper outlining plans for the annexation of Crimea.[^cite_note-Kim-111] It was reportedly part-written by Malofeev, and was published a year later by the Russian newspaper *[Novaya Gazeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novaya_Gazeta)*.[^cite_note-Novaya_predstavlyaetsya-112] The documents said that the Ukrainian government and president [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) were "totally bankrupt" and would not last.[^cite_note-Kim-111] They proposed that Russia should foster separatism in Crimea and other eastern provinces, and should begin work on taking control of them. The documents outlined plans for starting pro-Russian protests and a public relations campaign to justify a Russian takeover.[^cite_note-Kim-111][^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116]

On 21 February 2014, Ukrainian president Yanukovych secretly fled the capital. Russia's leadership worried that its naval base in Crimea might be at risk under a new Ukrainian government that was committed to closer ties with the West. On 22–23 February 2014,[^cite_note-117] Russian president Putin held an all-night meeting with security chiefs to discuss the crisis. At the end of that meeting, Putin said: "we must start working on returning Crimea to Russia".[^cite_note-auto-9][^cite_note-auto2-10][^cite_note-118]

The [GRU](261749.md) and [FSB](385990.md) then began organizing sympathizers in Crimea to ensure that when the operation began there would be armed "local self‑defense groups" on the streets for support.[^cite_note-galeotti-13]<sup>: 11 </sup> In January 2014, the city council of Sevastopol—home of Russia's naval base in Crimea—had called for the formation of "people's militia" units to "ensure firm defence" of the city from "extremism".[^cite_note-119]

According to political scientist [Daniel Treisman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Treisman), Putin may have begun planning to take Crimea even before Yanukovych was ousted. A source close to the commander of the operation said that Russian special forces in the port of [Novorossiysk](1190336.md) and the Black Sea Fleet in [Sevastopol](51587.md) had already been put on alert on 18 February. Participants in the operation were later awarded a [medal "For the Return of Crimea"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_%22For_the_Return_of_Crimea%22), which is engraved with 20 February as the start date of the operation.[^cite_note-120]

### Early unrest



"Crimean self-defence forces", 2 March 2014



A pro-Ukrainian demonstration in [Simferopol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simferopol) (Ukrainian flag on the left, Crimean Tatar flag on the right) during the Russian military intervention in Crimea, 9 March 2014

The February 2014 [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) that ousted Ukrainian president [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych) sparked a political crisis in Crimea, which initially manifested as [demonstrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2014_Crimean_crisis#23_February) against the new [interim Ukrainian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Yatsenyuk_Government),[^cite_note-crimea_1902-121] but rapidly escalated.

In late February, thousands of Russian soldiers dressed as civilians joined the "Crimean self-defense" units, as did almost one thousand [Kuban Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuban_Cossacks) from the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus). The activities of these groups were closely coordinated with Russian military forces and planned by Russian defense officials. Igor Girkin ('Strelkov') oversaw command of some of the units and their coordination with Russian security officials. Almost ten thousand people joined these "self-defense" units, but it is unclear how many were locals.[^cite_note-122]

On 20 February several buses with Crimean license plates were stopped at a pro-Maidan checkpoint in a town in [Cherkasy oblast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherkasy_oblast). Their passengers were violently intimidated and some buses were burned. This incident was subsequently used by Russian propaganda which made unsubstantiated claims that the passengers were killed in gruesome ways.[^cite_note-123]

The local parliament of Crimea was the [Verkhovna Rada of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verkhovna_Rada_of_Crimea) or Supreme Council of Crimea. Some MPs called for an extraordinary meeting on 21 February. In response to Russian separatist sentiment, the [Security Service of Ukraine](709222.md) (SBU) said that it would "use severe measures to prevent any action taken against diminishing the territorial integrity and sovereignty of Ukraine".[^cite_note-125] The party with the largest number of seats in the Crimean parliament (80 of 100), the [Party of Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Regions) of Ukrainian president Viktor Yanukovych, did not discuss Crimean secession, and were supportive of an agreement between President Yanukovych[^cite_note-yanukovych-91] and [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan) activists to end the unrest that was struck on the same day in [Kyiv](585629.md).[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127]

Crimean prime minister [Anatolii Mohyliov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolii_Mohyliov) said that his government recognised the new provisional government in Kyiv, and that the Crimean autonomous government would carry out all laws passed by the Ukrainian parliament.[^cite_note-128] In [Simferopol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simferopol), following a pro-Russian demonstration the previous day where protesters had replaced the Ukrainian flag over the parliament with a Russian flag,[^cite_note-129] a pro-Euromaidan rally of between 5,000 and 15,000 was held in support of the new government, and demanding the resignation of the Crimean parliament; attendees waved Ukrainian, Tatar, and European Union flags.[^cite_note-130] Meanwhile, in Sevastopol, thousands protested against the new Ukrainian government, voted to establish a parallel administration, and created civil defence squads with the support of the Russian [Night Wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_Wolves) motorcycle club. Protesters waved Russian flags, chanted "Putin is our president!" and said they would refuse to further pay taxes to the Ukrainian state.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-russiarally-132] Russian military convoys were also alleged to be seen in the area.[^cite_note-russiarally-132]

In [Kerch](47638.md), pro-Russian protesters attempted to remove the Ukrainian flag from atop city hall and replace it with the flag of Russia. Over 200 attended, waving Russian, [orange-and-black St. George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribbon_of_Saint_George), and the [Russian Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Unity) party flags. Mayor Oleh Osadchy attempted to disperse the crowd and police eventually arrived to defend the flag. The mayor said "This is the territory of Ukraine, Crimea. Here's a flag of Crimea," but was accused of treason and a fight ensued over the flagpole.[^cite_note-133] On 24 February, more rallied outside the Sevastopol city state administration.[^cite_note-134] Pro-Russian demonstrators accompanied by neo-[Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks) demanded the election of a Russian citizen as mayor and hoisted Russian flags around the city administration; they also handed out leaflets to sign up for a self-defence militia, warning that the "[Blue](9317.md)-[Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sturmabteilung) Europlague is knocking".[^cite_note-135]

[Volodymyr Yatsuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volodymyr_Yatsuba), head of Sevastopol administration, announced his resignation, citing the "decision of the city's inhabitants" made at a pro-Russian rally, and while caretaker city administration initially leaned towards recognition of new Ukrainian government,[^cite_note-136] continued pressure from pro-Russian activists forced local authorities to concede.[^cite_note-137] Consequently, [Sevastopol City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevastopol_City_Council) illegally elected [Alexei Chaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexei_Chaly), a Russian citizen, as mayor. Under the law of Ukraine, it was not possible for Sevastopol to elect a mayor, as the *Chairman of the Sevastopol City State Administration*, appointed by the [president of Ukraine](20688479.md), functions as its mayor.[^cite_note-YavlinskyUkr-138] A thousand protesters present chanted "A Russian mayor for a Russian city".[^cite_note-SevastopolInstalls-139]

On 25 February, several hundred pro-Russian protesters blocked the Crimean parliament demanding non-recognition of the central government of Ukraine and a referendum on Crimea's status.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] On the same day, crowds gathered again outside Sevastopol's city hall on Tuesday as rumours spread that security forces could arrest Chaly, but police chief Alexander Goncharov said that his officers would refuse to carry out "criminal orders" issued by Kyiv. Viktor Neganov, a Sevastopol-based adviser to the Internal Affairs Minister, condemned the events in the city as a coup. "Chaly represents the interests of the Kremlin which likely gave its tacit approval," he said. Sevastopol City State Administration chairman Vladimir Yatsuba was booed and heckled on 23 February, when he told a pro-Russian rally that Crimea was part of Ukraine. He resigned the next day.[^cite_note-SevastopolInstalls-139] In Simferopol, the Regional State Administration building was blockaded with hundreds of protesters, including neo-Cossacks, demanding a referendum of separation; the rally was organized by the *[Crimean Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Front)*.[^cite_note-143]



Protests outside the Crimean parliament building in Simferopol, 26 February 2014

On 26 February, outside the [Crimean parliament building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Building_of_the_Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) in Simferopol, 4,000–5,000 [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) and supporters of the [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan)-Crimea movement faced 600–700 supporters of pro-Russian organizations and the [Russian Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Unity) Party.[^cite_note-144] Tatars leaders organised the demonstration to block the sitting of the Crimean parliament which is "doing everything to execute plans of separation of Crimea from Ukraine". [Supreme Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) Chairman [Vladimir Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Konstantinov_\(politician\)) said that the Crimean parliament would not consider separation from Ukraine, and that earlier reports that parliament would hold a debate on the matter were provocations.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146] Tatars created self-defence groups, encouraged collaboration with Russians, Ukrainians, and people of other nationalities, and called for the protection of churches, mosques, synagogues, and other important sites.[^cite_note-147] By nightfall the Crimean Tatars had left; several hundred Russian Unity supporters rallied on.[^cite_note-148]

The [new Ukrainian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Yatsenyuk_Government)'s acting Internal Affairs Minister [Arsen Avakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsen_Avakov_\(politician\)) tasked Crimean law enforcement agencies not to provoke conflicts and to do whatever necessary to prevent clashes with pro-Russian forces; and he added "I think, that way – through a dialogue – we shall achieve much more than with standoffs".[^cite_note-149] New [Security Service of Ukraine](709222.md) (SBU) chief [Valentyn Nalyvaichenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valentyn_Nalyvaichenko) requested that the United Nations provide around-the-clock monitoring of the security situation in Crimea.[^cite_note-150]

### Russian military takeover of Crimea



Russian soldiers without markings in Simferopol, Crimea, 2 March 2014

Russia took advantage of the uncertainty in Ukraine immediately after the ousting of Yanukovych.[^cite_note-151] On 26 February, [Russian soldiers without insignia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_War\)) began to occupy Crimea and to blockade Ukrainian military bases.[^cite_note-CrimeanInvasion-152][^cite_note-DeBenedictis-153] In [Simferopol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simferopol), the Russian soldiers [seized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_the_Crimean_Parliament) the [Crimean parliament building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Building_of_the_Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) and the Council of Ministers building.[^cite_note-154] [Russian flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Russia) were raised over these buildings[^cite_note-155] and barricades were erected outside them.[^cite_note-LentaBerkut-156] A military checkpoint, with a Russian flag and Russian military vehicles, was set up on the main highway between Sevastopol and Simferopol.[^cite_note-globe26-157]



Russian soldiers without markings occupying the Crimean parliament building, 1 March 2014

While the armed soldiers occupied the Crimean parliament building, the parliament held an emergency session.[^cite_note-interfax27feb-158][^cite_note-reuters13mar-159] It voted to dismiss the Crimean government, and replace Prime Minister [Anatolii Mohyliov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolii_Mohyliov) with [Sergey Aksyonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Aksyonov).[^cite_note-The_Christian_Science_Monitor-160] Aksyonov belonged to the [Russian Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Unity) party, which received only 4% of votes in the last election.[^cite_note-reuters13mar-159] According to the [Constitution of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ukraine), the prime minister of Crimea is appointed by the Crimean parliament (Supreme Council of Crimea) in consultation with the president of Ukraine.[^cite_note-Yanu'snewPMC-161][^cite_note-162] Both Aksyonov and speaker [Vladimir Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Konstantinov_\(politician\)) stated that they viewed Viktor Yanukovych as the *de jure* president of Ukraine, through whom they were able to ask Russia for help.[^cite_note-163] The parliament also voted to hold a referendum on greater autonomy, set for 25 May. Historian [Andrew Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Wilson_\(historian\)) and journalist [Luke Harding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_Harding) called this the "Crimean [coup](25914154.md)".[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

The troops had cut all of the building's communications, and took MPs' phones as they entered.[^cite_note-interfax27feb-158][^cite_note-reuters13mar-159] No independent journalists were allowed inside the building while the votes were held.[^cite_note-reuters13mar-159] Some MPs said they were threatened and that votes were cast for them and other MPs, even though they were not in the chamber.[^cite_note-reuters13mar-159] [Interfax-Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfax-Ukraine) reported that there was no way to know how many MPs were present, and whether they voted themselves or if someone else used their voting cards.[^cite_note-IUCR27214-166] The head of parliament's information and analysis department, Olha Sulnikova, phoned journalists from inside the building, telling them 61 of the registered 64 deputies had voted for the referendum resolution and 55 for the resolution to dismiss the government.[^cite_note-IUCR27214-166] These votes were immediately declared illegal by the Ukrainian interim government.[^cite_note-167]

&#x20;Russian FSB colonel [Igor Girkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Girkin) (alias 'Strelkov'), one of the commanders of the soldiers, said in January 2015 that Crimean MPs were held at gunpoint, and were forced to support the annexation.[^cite_note-KP11MA2015-168] Girkin said:

> "Unfortunately I did not see any support from the \[Crimean] authorities in Simferopol where I was ... It was militants who collected deputies and forced them to vote. Yes, I was one of the commanders of those militants".[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170]



Armed men in *[Berkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkut_\(special_police_force\))* uniform guarding a checkpoint at [Chonhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chonhar), on the border of mainland Ukraine and Crimea, 10 March 2014

The same day, more unmarked troops set up [security checkpoints](8116691.md) on the [Isthmus of Perekop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus_of_Perekop) and the [Chonhar Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chonhar_Peninsula), which separate Crimea from the Ukrainian mainland.[^cite_note-LentaBerkut-156][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] They were helped by what appeared to be local *[Berkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkut_\(special_police_force\))* riot police, as well as Russian troops from the [31st Separate Airborne Assault Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/31st_Guards_Air_Assault_Brigade) dressed in Berkut uniforms.[^cite_note-175] Within hours, Ukraine had been cut off from Crimea. Ukrainian TV channels became unavailable for Crimean viewers, some replaced with Russian stations. On 29 February, unmarked Russian special forces occupied Crimea's airports and communications centers.[^cite_note-forceAuth-176]

On the day of the invasion, the Russian government dismissed accusations that it was in violation of the agreement on the [Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet): "All movements of armored vehicles are undertaken in full compliance with the basic agreements and did not require any approvals".[^cite_note-177] On 28 February, an estimated 1,400 Spetsnaz troops were flown in to the [Gvardeyskoye air base](72278570.md) from Russia.[^cite_note-178] On the same day, Putin assured German Chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel) in a phone call that no regular Russian soldiers are active in Crimea.[^cite_note-179]



The blockade of military units of the Armed Forces of Ukraine during the capture of Crimea by Russia in February–March 2014

On 1 March 2014, Aksyonov asked Putin for "assistance in ensuring peace and tranquillity" in Crimea.[^cite_note-180] Putin promptly received authorisation from the [Federation Council of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_Council_of_Russia) for a Russian military intervention in Ukraine until the "political-social situation in the country is normalized".[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-Federation_2014-182] Ukraine's prime minister, [Arseniy Yatsenyuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arseniy_Yatsenyuk), said that Russian military intervention would be the beginning of war,[^cite_note-forceAuth-176] and Ukraine's representative told an emergency meeting of the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) that Russia was committing "an [act of aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_aggression) against the state of Ukraine".[^cite_note-183] By 2 March, Russian troops were in full control of Crimea, having deployed from the naval base in Sevastopol and reinforced by troops, armour, and helicopters from mainland Russia.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-walker-the-guardian-2014-descend-185][^cite_note-bloomberg-news-2014-request-186]

At a press conference on 4 March, president Putin said that Russia had no plans to annex Crimea.[^cite_note-de-187] He also said that it had no plans to invade Ukraine, but that it might intervene if Russians in Ukraine were threatened.[^cite_note-de-187]

Numerous media reports and statements by the Ukrainian and foreign governments noted the identity of the unmarked troops as Russian soldiers, but Russian officials [concealed](2601678.md) the identity of their forces, claiming they were local "self-defence" units over whom they had no authority.[^cite_note-auto1-45] As late as 17 April, Russian foreign minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) said that there were no "excessive Russian troops" in Ukraine, referring to those who were allowed to be at the Sevastopol naval base.[^cite_note-188] Putin later acknowledged that he had ordered "work to bring Crimea back into Russia" as early as February.[^cite_note-revea-46]



Medal of the [Russian Defense Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Defense_Ministry) ["For the return of Crimea"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_%22For_the_Return_of_Crimea%22) ([Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language): За возвращение Крыма), 20 February – 18 March 2014

Russia eventually admitted its troops' presence.[^cite_note-189] Defence Minister [Sergey Shoygu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Shoygu) said Russian military actions in Crimea were undertaken by forces of the [Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet) and were justified by a "threat to lives of Crimean civilians" and danger of "takeover of Russian military infrastructure by extremists".[^cite_note-190] Ukraine complained that Russia violated the agreement under which it headquartered its Black Sea Fleet in Sevastopol[^cite_note-191] and violated the country's [sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-blogs.telegraph.co.uk-192] The United States and United Kingdom accused Russia of breaking the terms of the [Budapest Memorandum on Security Assurances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Memorandum_on_Security_Assurances), by which Russia, the US, and the UK had affirmed they would not use force against the [territorial integrity](31335.md) or political independence of Ukraine.[^cite_note-USStatement-193] The Russian government said the Budapest Memorandum[^cite_note-budapest-memorandum-194] did not apply due to "circumstances resulting from the action of internal political or socio-economic factors".[^cite_note-195] In March 2015, retired Russian Admiral [Igor Kasatonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Kasatonov?action=edit\&redlink=1) said that, according to his information, the Russian troop deployment in Crimea included six helicopter landings and three landings of an [IL-76](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IL-76) with 500 troops.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197]

The obligations between Russia and Ukraine with regard to [territorial integrity](31335.md) and the prohibition of the use of force are laid down in a number of multilateral or bilateral agreements to which Russia and Ukraine are signatories.[^cite_note-UkraineStatement-198][^cite_note-NATOStatement-199][^cite_note-USStatement-193][^cite_note-International_Law-200]

[Vladimir Putin](32817.md) said that Russian troops in the Crimean Peninsula were aimed "to ensure proper conditions for the people of Crimea to be able to freely express their will,"[^cite_note-201] whilst Ukraine and other nations argue that such intervention is a violation of [Ukraine's sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-blogs.telegraph.co.uk-192]

In the 1994 [Budapest Memorandum on Security Assurances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Memorandum_on_Security_Assurances)[^cite_note-budapest-memorandum-194] Russia was among those who affirmed to respect the territorial integrity of Ukraine (including Crimea) and to refrain from the threat or use of force against the territorial integrity or political independence of Ukraine.[^cite_note-USStatement-193][^cite_note-International_Law-200] The 1997 Russian–Ukrainian [Treaty on Friendship, Cooperation, and Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian%E2%80%93Ukrainian_Friendship_Treaty)[^cite_note-202] again reaffirmed the inviolability of the borders between both states,[^cite_note-International_Law-200] and required Russian forces in Crimea to respect the sovereignty of Ukraine, honor its legislation and not interfere in the internal affairs of the country.[^cite_note-BoundByTreaty-203]

The Russian–Ukrainian [Partition Treaty on the Status and Conditions of the Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_Treaty_on_the_Status_and_Conditions_of_the_Black_Sea_Fleet) signed in 1997 and prolonged in 2010, determined the status of Russian military presence in Crimea and restricted their operations,[^cite_note-International_Law-200] including requirement to show their "military identification cards" when crossing the international border and that operations beyond designated deployment sites was permitted only after coordination with Ukraine.[^cite_note-BoundByTreaty-203] According to Ukraine usage of navigation stations and troop movements were improperly covered by the treaty and were violated many times as well as related court decisions. February's troop movements were in "complete disregard" of the treaty.[^cite_note-Yeroshko-204][^cite_note-206]



"[Little green men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_\(Russo-Ukrainian_War\))" and trucks after the seizure of [Perevalne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perevalne) military base, 9 March 2014

According to the Constitution of Russia, the admission of new federal subjects is governed by federal constitutional law (art. 65.2).[^cite_note-207] Such a law was adopted in 2001, and it postulates that admission of a foreign state or its part into Russia shall be based on a mutual accord between the Russian Federation and the relevant state and shall take place pursuant to an international treaty between the two countries; moreover, it must be initiated by the state in question, not by its subdivision or by Russia.[^cite_note-208]

On 28 February 2014, Russian MP [Sergey Mironov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Mironov), along with other members of the [Duma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duma), introduced a bill to alter Russia's procedure for adding federal subjects. According to the bill, accession could be initiated by a subdivision of a country, provided that there is "absence of efficient sovereign state government in foreign state"; the request could be made either by subdivision bodies on their own or on the basis of a referendum held in the subdivision in accordance with corresponding national legislation.[^cite_note-209]

On 11 March 2014, both the [Supreme Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) and the [Sevastopol City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sevastopol_City_Council) adopted a [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_the_Republic_of_Crimea), which stated their intent to declare independence and request full accession to Russia should the pro-Russian option receive the most votes during the scheduled status referendum. The declaration directly referred to the [Kosovo independence precedent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_independence_precedent), by which the [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_people)-populated [Autonomous Province of Kosovo and Metohija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Province_of_Kosovo_and_Metohija) declared independence from Russia's ally [Serbia](29265.md) as the [Republic of Kosovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Kosovo) in 2008—a unilateral action Russia [staunchly opposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia's_reaction_to_the_2008_Kosovo_declaration_of_independence). The Russian government used [Kosovo independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_independence) precedent as a legal justification for the annexation of Crimea[^cite_note-auto3-210][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] Many analysts saw the Crimean declaration as an overt effort to pave the way for Crimea's annexation by Russia[^cite_note-214] and reject Russia's Kosovo precedent justification as being different compared to Crimea events,[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216] comparing the annexation to the [Nazi Germany](21212.md)'s [anschluss of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anschluss) and Czechoslovak [Sudetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Czechoslovakia_\(1938%E2%80%931945\)) instead.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]

Crimean authorities' stated plans to declare independence from Ukraine made the Mironov bill unnecessary. On 20 March 2014, two days after the [treaty of accession](#Accession_treaty_and_finalization_of_the_annexation) was signed, the bill was withdrawn by its initiators.[^cite_note-219]

At its meeting on 21–22 March, the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe)'s [Venice Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice_Commission) stated that the Mironov bill violated "in particular, the principles of territorial integrity, national sovereignty, non-intervention in the internal affairs of another state and [pacta sunt servanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacta_sunt_servanda)" and was therefore incompatible with [international law](8195726.md).[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]

### Crimean status referendum

Main article: [2014 Crimean status referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum)



A polling station during the "referendum"

On 27 February 2014, following the takeover of its building and replacement of Ukrainian-elected officials with Russian-controlled actors by Russian special forces,[^cite_note-\:3-222] the [Supreme Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) voted to hold a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum) on 25 May, with the initial question as to whether Crimea should upgrade its autonomy within Ukraine.[^cite_note-223] The referendum date was later moved from 25 May to 30 March.[^cite_note-224] A Ukrainian court declared the referendum to be illegal.[^cite_note-225]

On 6 March, the Supreme Council moved the referendum date to 16 March and changed its scope to ask a new question: whether Crimea should apply to join Russia as a [federal subject](701438.md) or restore the [1992 Crimean constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Crimean_constitution) within Ukraine, which the Ukrainian government had previously invalidated. This referendum, unlike one announced earlier, contained no option to maintain the *status quo* of governance under the 1998 constitution.[^cite_note-226] Ukraine's acting president, [Oleksandr Turchynov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleksandr_Turchynov), stated that "The authorities in Crimea are totally illegitimate, both the parliament and the government. They are forced to work under the barrel of a gun and all their decisions are dictated by fear and are illegal".[^cite_note-reutersgun-227]

On 14 March, the Crimean status referendum was deemed unconstitutional by the [Constitutional Court of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Ukraine),[^cite_note-228] and a day later, the [Verkhovna Rada](844993.md) formally dissolved the Crimean parliament.[^cite_note-zakon-48] With a referendum looming, Russia massed troops near the Ukrainian eastern border,[^cite_note-229] likely to threaten escalation and stymie Ukraine's response.

The referendum was held despite the opposition from the Ukrainian government. Official results reported about 95.5% of participating voters in Crimea (turnout was 83%) were in favour of seceding from Ukraine and joining Russia.[^cite_note-\:0-57][^cite_note-Collett-White-230][^cite_note-231] Crimean Tatars mostly boycotted the referendum.[^cite_note-232] A report by Evgeny Bobrov, a member of the [Russian President's Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Council_for_Civil_Society_and_Human_Rights), suggested the official results were inflated and between 50 and 60% of Crimeans voted for the reunification with Russia, with the turnout of 30–50%, meaning that 15% to 30% of Crimeans eligible to vote voted for the Russian annexation (the support was higher in administratively separate Sevastopol).[^cite_note-\:3-222][^cite_note-Washington_Post-47][^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] According to a survey carried out by [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in 2014, 54% of Crimean residents supported the right of regions to secede, 91% believed the referendum was free and fair and 88% believed that the government in Kyiv ought to recognize the results of the vote.[^cite_note-235]

The means by which the referendum was conducted were widely criticised by foreign governments[^cite_note-BBC_Voters-236] and in the Ukrainian and international press, with reports that anyone holding a Russian passport regardless of residency in Crimea was allowed to vote.[^cite_note-fast-237] [OSCE](22796.md) refused to send observers to the referendum, stating that invitation should have come from an OSCE member state in question (i.e. Ukraine), rather than local authorities.[^cite_note-238] Russia invited a group of observers from various European far-right political parties aligned with Putin, who stated the referendum was conducted in a free and fair manner.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240]

### Crimean proclamation of independence

"Republic of Crimea (country)" redirects here. For other uses, see [Republic of Crimea (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(disambiguation\)).

See also: [Declaration of Independence of the Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_the_Republic_of_Crimea)

The **Republic of Crimea** was short lived. On 17 March, following the official announcement of the [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Crimean_status_referendum) results, the [Supreme Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Council_of_Crimea) declared the formal independence of the Republic of Crimea, comprising the territories of both the [Autonomous Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Republic_of_Crimea) and the city of [Sevastopol](51587.md), which was granted [special status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_cities_of_Russia) within the breakaway republic.[^cite_note-res1745-241] The Crimean parliament declared the "partial repeal" of Ukrainian laws and began nationalising private and Ukrainian state property located on the Crimean Peninsula, including Ukrainian ports[^cite_note-242] and property of [Chornomornaftogaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chornomornaftogaz).[^cite_note-243] Parliament also formally requested that the [Russian government](9787373.md) admit the breakaway republic into Russia,[^cite_note-guardian20140317-244] with Sevastopol asking to be admitted as a "city of federal significance".[^cite_note-245] On the same day, the *de facto* Supreme Council renamed itself the [State Council of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Council_of_Crimea),[^cite_note-246] declared the [Russian ruble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ruble) an official currency alongside the [hryvnia](722696.md),[^cite_note-247] and in June the Russian ruble became the only form of legal tender.[^cite_note-248]

Putin officially recognised the Republic of Crimea 'as a sovereign and independent state' by [decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decree_of_the_President_of_Russia) on 17 March.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

On 21 March the Republic of Crimea became a federal [Subject of Russia](701438.md).

### Accession treaty

See also: [Republic of Crimea (Russia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(Russia\)), [Crimean Federal District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Federal_District), and [Political status of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Crimea)



President Putin with [Vladimir Konstantinov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Konstantinov_\(politician\)), [Sergey Aksyonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Aksyonov) and [Alexey Chaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexey_Chaly) at the Kremlin, 18 March 2014

The Treaty on Accession of the Republic of Crimea to Russia was signed between representatives of the Republic of Crimea (including Sevastopol, with which the rest of Crimea briefly unified) and the Russian Federation on 18 March 2014 to lay out terms for the immediate admission of the [Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(Russia\)) and [Sevastopol](51587.md) as [federal subjects of Russia](701438.md) and part of the Russian Federation.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-254] On 19 March, the [Russian Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Russia) decided that the treaty is in compliance with the [Constitution of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Russia).[^cite_note-255] The treaty was ratified by the [Federal Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_\(Russia\)) and [Federation Council](1268578.md) by 21 March.[^cite_note-256] [A Just Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Just_Russia)'s [Ilya Ponomarev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilya_Ponomarev) was the only [State Duma](1010187.md) member to vote against the treaty.[^cite_note-257] The [Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(country\)) and the [federal city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_city) of Sevastopol became the 84th and 85th [federal subjects of Russia](701438.md).[^cite_note-258]

Russian president Putin gave a [speech on 18 March 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_speech_of_Vladimir_Putin) to mark the annexation of Crimea. Putin defended the referendum as being "in full compliance with democratic procedures" and the principle of self-determination. He said "In people's hearts and minds, Crimea has always been an inseparable part of Russia" and argued that it should never have become part of an independent Ukraine. Putin lamented the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) for having "robbed" Russia of territories and making Russians "the biggest ethnic group in the world to be divided by borders", calling this an "outrageous historical injustice". According to Putin, the Ukrainian revolution was a "[coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup)" by "[Nationalists, neo-Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Ukraine), [Russophobes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russophobia) and [anti-Semites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Ukraine)". Referring to the armed men occupying Crimea as "local self-defense units", he claimed that "Russia's armed forces never entered Crimea", and he denied that Russia wanted to take any other regions of Ukraine.[^cite_note-259]

### Armed clash and withdrawal of Ukrainian troops

During [a controversial incident in Simferopol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Simferopol_incident) on 18 March, some Ukrainian sources said that armed gunmen that were reported to be Russian special forces allegedly stormed the base. This was contested by Russian authorities, who subsequently announced the arrest of an alleged Ukrainian sniper in connection with the killings,[^cite_note-260] but later denied the arrest had occurred.[^cite_note-261]

The two casualties had a joint funeral attended by both the Crimean and Ukrainian authorities, and both the [Ukrainian soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serhiy_Kokurin) and Russian paramilitary "self-defence volunteer" were mourned together.[^cite_note-262] As of March 2014 the incident was under investigation by both the Crimean authorities and the Ukrainian military.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

In response to shooting, Ukraine's then acting defense minister [Ihor Tenyukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ihor_Tenyukh) authorised Ukrainian troops stationed in Crimea to use deadly force in life-threatening situations. This increased the risk of bloodshed during any takeover of Ukrainian military installations, yet the ensuing Russian operations to seize the remaining Ukrainian military bases and ships in Crimea did not bring new fatalities, although weapons were used and several people were injured. The Russian units involved in such operations were ordered to avoid usage of deadly force when possible. Morale among the Ukrainian troops, which for three weeks were blockaded inside their compounds without any assistance from the Ukrainian government, was very low, and the vast majority of them did not offer any real resistance.[^cite_note-265]

On 24 March, the [Ukrainian government](1372585.md) ordered the full withdrawal of all of its armed forces from Crimea.[^cite_note-266] Approximately 50% of the Ukrainian soldiers in Crimea had defected to the Russian military.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] On 26 March the last Ukrainian military bases and [Ukrainian Navy](1590554.md) ships were captured by [Russian troops](25709.md).[^cite_note-140326_RcC-269]

### Continued occupation and integration with Russia

Further information: [Russian occupation of Crimea](71944314.md)



Russian troops in Crimea, March 2014

On 27 March, the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) adopted a [non-binding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-binding_resolution) [resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_68/262), which declared the Crimean referendum and subsequent status change invalid, by a vote of 100 to 11, with 58 abstentions and 24 absent.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

Crimea and Sevastopol switched to [Moscow Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Time) at 10 p.m. on 29 March.[^cite_note-Crimean_time_change-272][^cite_note-Time_switch-273]

On 31 March, Russia unilaterally [denounced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denunciation_\(international_law\)) the [Kharkiv Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharkiv_Pact)[^cite_note-274] and [Partition Treaty on the Status and Conditions of the Black Sea Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_Treaty_on_the_Status_and_Conditions_of_the_Black_Sea_Fleet).[^cite_note-275] Putin cited "the accession of the [Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(Russia\)) and Sevastopol into Russia" and resulting "practical end of [renting](342324.md) relationships" as his reason for the denunciation.[^cite_note-276] On the same day, he signed a decree formally rehabilitating the [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars), who were [ousted from their lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_the_Crimean_Tatars) in 1944, and the Armenian, German, Greek, and Bulgarian minority communities in the region that [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Stalin) also ordered removed in the 1940s.

Also on 31 March 2014, the Russian prime minister [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) announced a series of programmes aimed at swiftly incorporating the territory of Crimea into Russia's economy and infrastructure. Medvedev announced the creation of a new ministry for Crimean affairs, and ordered Russia's top ministers who joined him there to make coming up with a development plan their top priority.[^cite_note-277] On 3 April 2014, the Republic of Crimea and the city of [Sevastopol](51587.md) became parts of Russia's [Southern Military District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Military_District). On 7 May 2015, Crimea switched its phone code system from the [Ukrainian number system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Ukraine) to the [Russian number system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Russia).[^cite_note-278]



Russian forces during Victory Day in Sevastopol, 2014

On 11 April, the [Constitution of the Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Republic_of_Crimea) and City Charter of Sevastopol were adopted by their respective legislatures,[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] coming into effect the following day in addition the new federal subjects were enumerated in a newly published revision of the Russian Constitution.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282]

On 14 April, [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) announced that he would open a ruble-only account with [Bank Rossiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rossiya_Bank) and would make it the primary bank in the newly annexed Crimea as well as giving the right to service payments on Russia's \$36 billion wholesale electricity market – which gave the bank \$112 million annually from commission charges alone.[^cite_note-283]

Russia withdrew its forces from southern [Kherson](1025861.md) in December 2014.[^cite_note-284]

In December 2014, and electricity supply agreement was made between Ukraine's [Ukrinterenergo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrinterenergo) and Russia's [Inter RAO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter_RAO) that electricity would be supplied from Ukraine in return for an equivalent supply of Russian electricity to Ukraine, which resolved electricity rationing in Crimea.[^cite_note-osw-20151125-285]

In July 2015, Russian prime minister, [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev), declared that Crimea had been fully integrated into Russia.[^cite_note-286] Until 2016 these new subjects were grouped in the [Crimean Federal District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Federal_District).



Victory Day in Simferopol, occupied Crimea, 2019

In 2017, a survey performed by the Centre for East European and International Studies showed that 85% of the non-Crimean Tatar respondents believed that if the referendum would be held again it would lead to the same or "only marginally different" results. Crimea was fully integrated into the Russian media sphere, and links with the rest of Ukraine were hardly existent.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288]

On 28 December 2018, Russia completed a high-tech security fence marking the de facto border between Crimea and Ukraine.[^cite_note-289]

In 2021, Ukraine launched the [Crimea Platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimea_Platform), a diplomatic initiative aimed at protecting the rights of Crimean inhabitants and ultimately reversing the annexation of Crimea.[^cite_note-290]

## Transition and aftermath

### Economic implications

See also: [Economic history of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_Russian_Federation) and [Russian financial crisis (2014–2016)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_financial_crisis_\(2014%E2%80%932016\))



A Ukrainian [Chornomornaftogaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chornomornaftogaz) platform in the Black Sea, which Russia seized during the Crimea annexation

Initially after the annexation, salaries rose, especially those of government workers. This was soon offset by the increase in prices caused by the [depreciation of the ruble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_Russian_financial_crisis). Wages were cut back by 30% to 70% after Russian authority became established. Tourism, previously Crimea's main industry, suffered in particular, down by 50% from 2014 in 2015.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] Crimean agricultural yields were also significantly impacted by the annexation. Ukraine cut off supplies of water through the [North Crimean Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Crimean_Canal), which supplies 85% of Crimea's fresh water, causing the 2014 rice crop to fail, and greatly damaging the maize and [soybean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean) crops.[^cite_note-6ME-293] The annexation had a negative influence on Russians working in Ukraine and Ukrainians working in Russia.[^cite_note-294]

The number of tourists visiting Crimea in the 2014 season was lower than in the previous years due to a combination of "Western sanctions", ethical objections by Ukrainians, and the difficulty of getting there for Russians.[^cite_note-295] The Russian government attempted to stimulate the flow of tourists by subsidizing holidays in the peninsula for children and state workers from all Russia[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] which worked mostly for state-owned hotels. In 2015, overall 3 million tourists visited Crimea according to official data, while before annexation it was around 5.5 million on average. The shortage is attributed mostly to stopped flow of tourists from Ukraine. Hotels and restaurants are also experiencing problems with finding enough seasonal workers, who were most arriving from Ukraine in the preceding years. Tourists visiting state-owned hotels were complaining mostly about low standard of rooms and facilities, some of them still unrepaired from Soviet times.[^cite_note-298]

According to the German newspaper *[Die Welt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Welt)*, the annexation of Crimea is economically disadvantageous for the Russian Federation. Russia will have to spend billions of euros a year to pay salaries and pensions. Moreover, Russia will have to undertake costly projects to connect Crimea to the Russian water supply and power system because Crimea has no land connection to Russia and at present (2014) gets water, gas and electricity from mainland Ukraine. This required building [a bridge](22563317.md) and a pipeline across the [Kerch Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerch_Strait). Also, Novinite claims that a Ukrainian expert told *Die Welt* that Crimea "will not be able to attract tourists".[^cite_note-299]

The then first Deputy to [Minister of Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance_\(Russia\)) of Russian Federation [Tatyana Nesterenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatyana_Nesterenko) said that the decision to annex Crimea was made by Vladimir Putin exclusively, without consulting [Russia's Finance Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance_\(Russia\)).[^cite_note-300]

The Russian [business newspaper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Russia) *[Kommersant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kommersant)* expresses an opinion that Russia will not acquire anything economically from "accessing" Crimea, which is not very developed industrially, having just a few big factories, and whose yearly gross product is only \$4 billion. The newspaper also says that everything from Russia will have to be delivered by sea, higher costs of transportation will result in higher prices for everything, and to avoid a decline in living standards Russia will have to subsidise Crimean people for a few months. In total, Kommersant estimates the costs of integrating Crimea into Russia in \$30 billion over the next decade, i.e. \$3 billion per year.[^cite_note-kommersant20140207-301]

Western oil expertsestimate that Russia's seizing of Crimea, and the associated control of an area of [Black Sea](3386.md) more than three times its land area gives it access to oil and gas reserves potentially worth trillions of dollars.[^cite_note-302] It also deprives Ukraine of its chances of energy independence. Moscow's acquisition may alter the route along which the [South Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Stream) pipeline would be built, saving Russia money, time and engineering challenges. It would also allow Russia to avoid building in Turkish territorial waters, which was necessary in the original route to avoid Ukrainian territory.[^cite_note-In_taking_Crimea,_Putin_gains_a_sea_of_fuel_reserves-303] This pipeline was later canceled in favour of [TurkStream](45206475.md), however.



Vladimir Putin and his close confidant [Arkady Rotenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkady_Rotenberg) before the opening of the [Crimean Bridge](22563317.md) in May 2018

The [Russian Federal Service for Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Service_for_Supervision_of_Communications,_Information_Technology_and_Mass_Media) (Roskomnadzor) warned about a transition period as Russian operators have to change the numbering capacity and subscribers. [Country code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_code) will be replaced from the [Ukrainian +380](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Ukraine) to [Russian +7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Russia). Codes in Crimea start with [65](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_dialling_codes_in_Ukraine#65_%E2%80%93_Crimea), but in the area of ["7"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Soviet_Union) the 6 is given to Kazakhstan which shares former Soviet Union +7 with Russia, so city codes have to change. The regulator assigned 869 [dialling code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Russia) to Sevastopol and the rest of the peninsula received a 365 code.[^cite_note-304] At the time of the unification with Russia, telephone operators and Internet service providers in Crimea and Sevastopol are connected to the outside world through the territory of Ukraine.[^cite_note-305] [Minister of Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Communications_and_Mass_Media_\(Russia\)) of Russia, [Nikolai Nikiforov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Nikiforov) announced on his Twitter account that [postal codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_codes_in_Russia) in Crimea will now have six-figures: to the existing five-digit number the number two will be added at the beginning. For example, the Simferopol postal code 95000 will become 295000.[^cite_note-306]

In the area that now forms the border between Crimea and Ukraine mining the [salt lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_lake) inlets from the sea that constitute the natural borders, and in the spit of land left over stretches of no-man's-land with wire on either side was created.[^cite_note-307] On early June that year Prime Minister [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) signed a [Government resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Government_of_Russia) No.961[^cite_note-308] dated 5 June 2014 establishing air, sea, road and railway checkpoints. The adopted decisions create a legal basis for the functioning of a checkpoint system at the [Russian state border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_state_border) in the Republic of Crimea and Sevastopol.[^cite_note-309]

In the year following the annexation, armed men seized various Crimean businesses, including banks, hotels, shipyards, farms, gas stations, a bakery, a dairy, and Yalta Film Studio.[^cite_note-AP141202-310][^cite_note-NYT150110-311][^cite_note-AFP150227-312] Russian media have noted this trend as "returning to the 90's," which is perceived as a period of anarchy and rule of gangs in Russia.[^cite_note-313]

After 2014 the Russian government invested heavily in the peninsula's infrastructure—repairing roads, modernizing hospitals and building the [Crimean Bridge](22563317.md) that links the peninsula to the Russian mainland. Development of new sources of water was undertaken, with huge difficulties, to replace closed Ukrainian sources.[^cite_note-314] In 2015, the Investigative Committee of Russia announced a number of theft and corruption cases in infrastructure projects in Crimea, for example; spending that exceeded the actual accounted costs by a factor of three. A number of Russian officials were also arrested for corruption, including head of federal tax inspection.[^cite_note-315]

(According to February 2016 official Ukrainian figures) after Russia's annexation 10% of [Security Service of Ukraine](709222.md) personnel left Crimea; accompanied by 6,000 of the pre-annexation 20,300 people strong [Ukrainian army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_army).[^cite_note-316]

As result of the disputed political status of Crimea, Russian mobile operators never expanded their operations into Crimea and all mobile services are offered on the basis of "internal roaming," which caused significant controversy inside Russia. Telecoms however argued that expanding coverage to Crimea will put them at risk of Western sanctions and, as result, they will lose access to key equipment and software, none of which is produced locally.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]

The first five years of Crimean occupation cost Russia over \$20 billion, roughly equal to two years of Russia's entire education budget.[^cite_note-319]

### Human rights situation



A commemoration in Kyiv likening the [Deportation of the Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_the_Crimean_Tatars) to the annexation of Crimea

According to the United Nations and multiple [NGOs](46539.md), Russia is responsible for multiple human rights abuses, including torture, [arbitrary detention](13435705.md), [forced disappearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_disappearance) and instances of discrimination, including persecution of [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Tatars) in Crimea since the illegal annexation.[^cite_note-urlCrimea:_Persecution_of_Crimean_Tatars_Intensifies|_Human_Rights_Watch-320][^cite_note-321] The [UN Human Rights Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OHCHR) has documented multiple human rights violations in Crimea. Noting that minority Crimean Tatars have been disproportionately affected.[^cite_note-OHCHR18-322][^cite_note-OHCHR20-323] In December 2016, the [UN General Assembly](31957.md) voted on a resolution on human rights in occupied Crimea. It called on the Russian Federation "to take all measures necessary to bring an immediate end to all abuses against residents of Crimea, in particular reported discriminatory measures and practices, arbitrary detentions, torture and other cruel, inhumane or degrading treatment, and to revoke all discriminatory legislation". It also urged Russia to "immediately release Ukrainian citizens who were unlawfully detained and judged without regard for elementary standards of justice".[^cite_note-324]

After the annexation, Russian authorities banned Crimean Tatar organizations, filed criminal charges against Tatar leaders and journalists, and targeted the Tatar population.
